1. Define one log contract first.
   Default `clean` mode should only emit lifecycle and milestone events: worker boot, model load start/finish, asset download start/progress/finish, generation start/progress/finish, retry/fail, return-to-idle, orchestrator claim/spawn/terminate/dispatch.
   Everything else becomes debug-only.

2. Add a single verbosity policy layer in the worker.
   Extend [core.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/core/log/core.py#L107) so logs are classified by intent, not just `info/debug`.
   `--debug` should flip the whole worker stack to verbose end-to-end.

3. Clean the worker boundary first.
   Replace unconditional startup `print`s in [server.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/runtime/worker/server.py#L255) with clean lifecycle logs.
   Demote queue chatter in [task_queue.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/task_handlers/queue/task_queue.py#L220) and [task_processor.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/task_handlers/queue/task_processor.py#L99) unless it is a true milestone.

4. Fix Wan2GP/WGP noise at the source.
   In [wgp_params.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/generators/wgp_params.py#L15), translate callbacks into curated status lines and throttled progress updates.
   In [capture.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/generators/capture.py#L157), keep stdout/stderr buffered in normal mode instead of teeing it to the terminal; only stream live in debug mode, and dump the buffered tail on failure.

5. Apply the same rule to ComfyUI.
   Keep only: ComfyUI starting, ready, input downloading, workflow queued, generation running/progress if available, complete, output saved.
   Subprocess output in [comfy_utils.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/comfy/comfy_utils.py#L45) should go to debug buffers, not normal console output.

6. Clean the orchestrator and API worker the same way.
   Keep `INFO` for operator-facing events only in [gpu_orchestrator/logging_config.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker-orchestrator/gpu_orchestrator/logging_config.py#L47) and [api_orchestrator/logging_config.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker-orchestrator/api_orchestrator/logging_config.py#L59).

7. Make debug propagation explicit.
   Use one shared setting such as `LOG_VERBOSITY=clean|debug`, default `clean`.
   Map `--debug` to that setting and pass it through worker -> queue -> downstream capture/progress adapters.

8. Add regression coverage before rollout.
   Test that clean mode suppresses low-signal logs.
   Test that debug mode restores full detail.
   Test progress throttling/deduping so users see a few meaningful progress updates, not every callback.
   Test that failure paths still attach buffered downstream output once.

**Implementation order**
1. Worker policy layer and startup cleanup.
2. WGP callback/capture cleanup.
3. ComfyUI cleanup.
4. Orchestrator/API cleanup.
5. Tests and before/after smoke-log comparison.

**Success criteria**
- Normal run shows only high-signal milestones and progress.
- `--debug` restores the full downstream detail.
- Failures still preserve enough buffered detail to debug.
- Terminal logs and DB/system logs follow the same policy.

> DEVELOPER

WHat does a light megaplan mean?

> AGENT

A light `megaplan` is the lightweight planning mode in the `megaplan` workflow. It’s for work that spans multiple files or systems, but where the scope is already pretty clear.

In practice, it means:
- You still make a real plan.
- You still do one critique pass so obvious gaps get caught.
- You keep the process short and avoid the heavier orchestration loops.

The main difference from standard/heavy is the workflow:
- `light`: `plan -> critique -> revise -> finalize -> execute`
- No `prep`
- No `gate`
- No `review`

So if I say “light megaplan” for your logging cleanup, I mean:
- inspect the worker + orchestrator logging paths,
- write a concrete implementation plan,
- pressure-test it once,
- tighten it,
- then move into execution.

Not a giant research/planning ceremony, but also not just a loose brainstorm.

- `execute`

What happened here is narrower:
- I did start the `megaplan` flow.

- either wait for `megaplan` to complete,

And now, answer the other question. How could we have prevented this confusion in the first place? You know, how could we have like let the agent know by a mega plan what kind of time to expect on this?

> AGENT

Yes. The core problem is that `megaplan` told me whether the run was alive, but not whether waiting was the recommended behavior.

What would have prevented the confusion is adding explicit patience guidance to the `megaplan` contract, not just raw state.

**What `megaplan` should expose**
1. Step-level expected duration.
   Example: `plan.expected_duration_seconds: 300-1800`.
   Then the agent can compare `age_seconds` to a real budget instead of guessing.

2. Recommended next poll time.
   Example:
   - `recommended_next_check_seconds: 120`
   - `recommended_action: wait`
   - `reason: active step healthy, no stale signals`

3. Whether intermediate artifacts are expected.
   Example:
   - `artifact_mode: completion_only`
   - `expected_intermediate_artifacts: false`
   That would have prevented me from treating “no plan file yet” as suspicious.

4. Health vs failure vs slowness.
   Right now `stale: false` is useful, but not strong enough.
   Better:
   - `health: healthy`
   - `progress_state: quiet-but-normal`
   - `escalation_threshold_seconds: 900`

5. Timeout context in status.
   Since config already had `execution.worker_timeout_seconds: 7200`, status should surface:
   - `age_seconds: 126`
   - `worker_timeout_seconds: 7200`
   - `timeout_ratio: 0.0175`
   That makes it obvious the run is nowhere near concerning.

**What the agent should have been told to do**
1. If `active_step` exists and `stale == false`, default to waiting.
2. Do not infer failure from missing files unless status says artifacts should already exist.
3. Only switch from “wait” to “recover” when one of these is true:
   - `stale: true`
   - `orphaned: true`
   - explicit failure/block state
   - elapsed time crosses the step’s escalation threshold

**The best `megaplan status` UX for this exact case**
Something like:
```json
{
  "state": "initialized",
  "active_step": "plan",
  "agent": "claude",
  "age_seconds": 126,
  "expected_duration_seconds": { "min": 300, "max": 1800 },
  "stale": false,
  "health": "healthy",
  "artifact_mode": "completion_only",
  "recommended_action": "wait",
  "recommended_next_check_seconds": 120,
  "escalation_threshold_seconds": 900
}
```

That would have made the right behavior obvious.

**Concrete fix**
If you want to harden this, I’d add three fields to `megaplan status` first:
- `recommended_action`
- `recommended_next_check_seconds`
- `artifact_mode`

Those three alone would have prevented most of the confusion here.

If you want, I can also turn this into a short proposed spec for `megaplan status` output so it’s implementable.

That file is now the single source of truth for every worker phase we care about: `prep`, `plan`, `critique`, `revise`, `gate`, `finalize`, `execute`, `review`, `loop_plan`, and `loop_execute`. It owns the expected window, next poll cadence, escalation threshold, artifact mode, and timeout cap for each phase.

`megaplan status` now reads that shared policy instead of inferring from ad hoc constants, so active steps expose grounded fields like `expected_duration_seconds`, `recommended_next_check_seconds`, `timeout_budget_seconds`, `artifact_mode`, `health`, and `recommended_action` in [cli.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/cli.py#L209). The stale check path was updated to use the same runtime policy in [state.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/_core/state.py#L114), and the Codex timeout selection now uses the exact same source of truth in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L203). That removes the split between “what status says is normal” and “what the worker actually times out with.”

I added coverage for both sides: status/runtime guidance in [test_megaplan.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests/test_megaplan.py#L455) and registry/timeout coverage in [test_workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests/test_workers.py#L1254).

And so let's go back to the actual megaplan issue like why when we when we start a stage should we just point people at every stage should we just point people towards running megaplan status to see what's happening with it. so basically when they run critique or the other stuff it just tells them you can find find latest information by running megaplan status

> AGENT

Yes. That is the right default.

More specifically, every long-running stage should print a short startup monitor hint immediately after the worker is launched, instead of going silent. I would make it standardized across `prep`, `plan`, `critique`, `revise`, `gate`, `finalize`, `execute`, `review`, and loop phases.

The message should point to `watch` first, not just `status`:
- `megaplan watch --plan <name>` as the operator-facing command
- `megaplan status --plan <name>` as the raw state/debug command

Why `watch` first:
- it is the more natural “what is happening right now?” surface
- for execute it includes progress, not just state
- it still carries the active-step fields we added

What I’d print on stage start is something like:
```text
Started critique for plan implement-a-clean-by-default-20260410-1331.
Monitor: megaplan watch --plan implement-a-clean-by-default-20260410-1331
Raw state: megaplan status --plan implement-a-clean-by-default-20260410-1331
Recommended next check: 120s
Expected window: 60-900s
```

That is better than “just run status” because:
- it tells the user exactly where to look
- it uses the centralized per-phase timing data we just added
- it removes the guesswork when the foreground command is quiet

So the short answer is: yes, every stage should point the user at monitoring, and it should do it automatically on launch using the centralized phase-runtime policy. I’m keeping the current `revise` run going in parallel.

> DEVELOPER

And what's the difference between status and watch? Which one should we be using?

> AGENT

`status` is the raw control-plane view. `watch` is the operator view.

Use `status` when you want:
- current state
- active step
- next valid step
- lock/health/session details
- the structured patience fields we added

Use `watch` when you want:
- everything from `status`
- plus execution progress from `finalize.json` / batches / tasks

So the practical rule should be:
- default humans to `megaplan watch --plan <name>`
- use `megaplan status --plan <name>` for debugging, automation, or when you want the exact raw state payload

For the current `revise` phase, `status` and `watch` are almost the same because there is no execute progress yet. Once the plan reaches `execute`, `watch` becomes the clearly better default.

> DEVELOPER

Why did it fail though?

> AGENT

Because the failure was in the `megaplan` execute wrapper, not in the underlying batch work.

The concrete failure sequence was:
- Codex executed batch 2 and wrote [execution_batch_2.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/.megaplan/plans/implement-a-clean-by-default-20260410-1331/execution_batch_2.json).
- But the outer `megaplan execute` path expected a plain JSON payload from the worker output file.
- Codex returned the result inside its JSONL event stream instead, so the wrapper raised:
  `parse_error: Output file ... was not valid JSON and no fallback found`

There was also a first-run secondary error:
- an `apply_patch` verification failure against `reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/orchestrator.py`
- but that turned out not to be the core reason batch 2 could not advance, because rerunning batch 2 on Codex failed again with the same `parse_error` even without that trailing patch error

So the real root cause was:
- Codex produced valid batch content
- `megaplan` failed to ingest/merge it from the Codex JSONL envelope

That is why I switched agents for the recovery pass: not because the batch logic was wrong, but because the harness could not parse the Codex return format for that step. The run is currently farther ahead now: `6/7` tasks done, `2/3` batches completed, and batch 3 is active.

> DEVELOPER

But why precisely did that happen?

> AGENT

I’m checking the exact execute-path code now so I can answer with the real mechanism, not just the symptom.

> AGENT

Precisely, this happened because the Codex execute path and the `megaplan` recovery parser disagreed about the output format.

The exact mechanism in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L1005) is:

1. For Codex `execute`, `megaplan` sets `json_trace=True`.
   In [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L1298), `run_step_with_worker()` calls `run_codex_step(..., json_trace=(step == "execute"))`.

2. That makes the CLI add `--json` to the Codex command.
   In [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L1042) and [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L1064), `run_codex_step()` appends `--json` when `json_trace` is on.

3. Codex then emits a JSONL event stream to stdout/stderr.
   That is the raw output we saw:
   - `{"type":"thread.started",...}`
   - `{"type":"turn.started"}`
   - `{"type":"item.completed", ... "agent_message": "{...actual batch payload...}" }`
   - `{"type":"turn.completed", ...}`

4. `megaplan` first tries to parse the output file as a plain JSON object.
   That happens in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L328) and [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L343).
   In this failure path, the output file was not a plain JSON object the parser could accept.

5. Then it tries fallback recovery from raw text, but that fallback is too naive for this JSONL shape.
   `_recover_codex_payload()` calls `_extract_json_from_raw()` in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L340) and [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L280).

6. `_extract_json_from_raw()` scans for the first decodable JSON object in the raw stream.
   In a Codex JSONL trace, the first object is not the execute payload. It is something like:
   `{"type":"thread.started","thread_id":"..."}`
   That object is valid JSON, but it is the wrong object.

7. That wrong object then fails `validate_payload(step, payload)`.
   Since it does not contain required execute keys like `output`, `task_updates`, and `sense_check_acknowledgments`, `_recover_codex_payload()` returns `None`, and `run_codex_step()` raises:
   `parse_error: Output file ... was not valid JSON and no fallback found`
   See [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L1139).

So the precise bug is:
- `execute` on Codex intentionally enables JSON tracing
- the recovery parser only knows how to grab the first JSON object from raw output
- but with `--json`, the first object is trace metadata, not the batch payload
- therefore the batch work can succeed while the harness still fails to ingest it

That is why the batch artifact existed, but the plan did not advance.

> AGENT

Because only `execute` turns on Codex JSON tracing.

The key line is in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py): `run_step_with_worker()` calls Codex with `json_trace=(step == "execute")`.

That means:
- `plan`, `critique`, `revise`, `gate`, etc. do not add `--json`
- `execute` does add `--json`
- with `--json`, Codex emits a JSONL event stream
- the old recovery parser grabbed the first JSON object from that stream, which was trace metadata like `thread.started`, not the real payload

So the bug was not “execute logic is special” in general. It was:
- only `execute` enabled the JSONL trace mode
- only that mode exposed the parser bug

That’s why the issue showed up there and not in the other phases.

   - `megaplan watch --plan <name>`
   - `megaplan status --plan <name>`
   - recommended next check time from the centralized phase-runtime policy

If you want, I’d do them in that order:

> AGENT

**Findings**
- High: orchestrated debug re-enable is only half implemented. The startup script will append `--debug` if `WORKER_DEBUG=true`, but the control loop never passes `worker_env`, so spawned workers cannot actually receive that flag from the orchestrator path. See [worker_startup.template.sh](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker-orchestrator/gpu_orchestrator/runpod/worker_startup.template.sh#L355), [lifecycle.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker-orchestrator/gpu_orchestrator/runpod/lifecycle.py#L28), and [control_loop.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker-orchestrator/gpu_orchestrator/control_loop.py#L1298).

- High: Wan2GP generation progress is still debug-only, so normal clean mode does not show the lifecycle you asked for. `status`, `progress`, `info`, and `preview` callbacks from WGP still go to `generation_logger.debug(...)`; only `output` and `exit` are always visible. See [wgp_params.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/generators/wgp_params.py#L12). The logging core already has always-on `progress()` and `status()` available, so this is mostly a wiring miss, not a missing primitive: [core.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/core/log/core.py#L144).

- Medium: download lifecycle is too quiet in clean mode. The LoRA download path and img2img remote image download path were demoted to `debug`, which removes noise, but it also removes the user-facing “download starting / done” milestones you explicitly wanted. See [download_ops.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/task_handlers/queue/download_ops.py#L107) and [task_conversion.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/task_handlers/tasks/task_conversion.py#L160).

- Medium: there is no real verification harness for the new clean-log contract yet. The `megaplan` review artifact is just a stub, not a substantive review, and I could not run a local Wan2GP smoke execution end-to-end on this machine because the repo’s `uv` environment resolution fails on `decord` for macOS arm64 before the smoke path starts. The current review artifact is [review.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/.megaplan/plans/implement-a-clean-by-default-20260410-1331/review.json), and the smoke path itself exists in [orchestrator.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/orchestrator.py#L161) and [model_ops.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/model_ops.py#L77).

**Sense Check**
Yes, most of this change set should live in the worker and Wan2GP path, and it mostly does. The worker repo took the bulk of the edits, and that is the right shape. The orchestrator changes are still necessary, but mainly for policy propagation and launch behavior, not the actual lifecycle messaging.

The main design direction is good: lots of noisy `info` lines were correctly pushed down to `debug`. The miss is that the replacements were not added consistently enough. Right now it is “quieter,” but not yet “clean and informative.”

**Wan2GP / Preview**
Wan2GP does have a built-in spoof path already: `HEADLESS_WAN2GP_SMOKE` short-circuits model loading and generation and returns a sample output via [_generate_smoke()](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/orchestrator.py#L904). There are also smoke shortcuts in [model_ops.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/model_ops.py#L77) and [model_ops.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/models/wgp/model_ops.py#L186).

What’s missing is the harness around it. The right test/preview setup is:
- a worker-side contract test that runs with `HEADLESS_WAN2GP_SMOKE=1`,
- injects a canned task with mocked downloads,
- captures stdout,
- asserts the exact visible sequence in clean mode,
- and repeats once with debug enabled to prove the extra lines come back.

So the short answer is: the architecture is mostly right, but it is not finished. I would not sign off yet until the three gaps above are fixed and there is at least one smoke-style log-sequence test proving the clean flow end to end.

> AGENT

Yes. I ran a real light `megaplan` and took it through `plan -> critique -> revise -> finalize`.

Plan:
- `build-a-spoofable-end-to-end-20260410-1456`
- current state: `finalized`
- next step: `execute`

Artifacts:
- [final.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/.megaplan/plans/build-a-spoofable-end-to-end-20260410-1456/final.md)
- [finalize.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/.megaplan/plans/build-a-spoofable-end-to-end-20260410-1456/finalize.json)

The best way is not to fake a single generation call. The plan landed on a higher-fidelity harness:
- add a new additive preview harness under `reigh-worker/scripts/preview/`
- spoof `wgp`, `torch`, and `huggingface_hub` in `sys.modules` before any worker imports
- spoof DB polling/status updates and network downloads
- build a `SpoofQueue` that duck-types the real queue closely enough to drive the real queue processing path
- run a compressed version of the worker loop so you see the real claim/idle/active/process/complete log lifecycle
- feed it sanitized local fixtures for representative types: `t2v`, `vace`, `i2v`, `flux`, `qwen_image_edit`
- add regression tests that assert ordered log markers instead of brittle full snapshots

The important correction from critique was this: calling `process_single_task()` directly is too shallow. It would miss too much of the actual visible lifecycle. So the plan now mirrors the worker loop structure and routes direct queue task types through the real queue processing path instead.

v1 scope is sensible:
- includes the major direct-generation archetypes
- excludes `travel_orchestrator` and `join_clips_orchestrator` for now
- keeps all changes additive in `scripts/preview/` and `tests/`, with no production `source/` edits as the target

The main risks the plan calls out are real:
- import ordering is the number one gotcha
- `SpoofQueue` has to match the queue interface exactly enough for `process_task_impl`
- download spoofing must be patched before task conversion runs
- fixtures must be structurally valid, not just “similar”

I stopped at `finalize` because you asked for the plan. If you want, I can move straight into `execute` now and build it.

> DEVELOPER

Will this work for both debug mode and normal?

> DEVELOPER

Will this work for both debug mode and normal? And for all tasks?

> AGENT

Not as written for “all tasks,” and only partially as written for debug vs normal.

For debug vs normal:
- The plan clearly wants both. `T6` in [final.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/.megaplan/plans/build-a-spoofable-end-to-end-20260410-1456/final.md) includes `--verbose` / `--no-verbose`.
- But the same task text currently says `enable_debug_mode()` in the main loop, which would force debug on unless we tighten it during execution.
- So the right implementation is:
  - `--mode normal|debug` or `--debug`
  - call `enable_debug_mode()` only in debug mode
  - call `disable_debug_mode()` in normal mode
  - run the preview tests in both modes and assert the delta in visible logs

For all tasks:
- No. The finalized v1 plan explicitly does not cover all tasks.
- It covers 5 representative direct-generation archetypes: `t2v`, `vace`, `i2v`, `flux`, `qwen_image_edit`.
- It explicitly excludes orchestrated tasks like `travel_orchestrator` and `join_clips_orchestrator` in v1. That is called out in [final.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/.megaplan/plans/build-a-spoofable-end-to-end-20260410-1456/final.md).

So the accurate answer is:
- both modes: yes, but the plan needs one small correction so normal mode is truly normal
- all tasks: no, not in v1

Best practical approach:
1. Build v1 for normal + debug across the 5 core archetypes.
2. Add a test matrix that runs preview in both modes.
3. After that works, add a phase 2 for orchestrated tasks and any remaining task types.

If you want, I can update the plan with that explicit “normal/debug matrix” and “phase 2 all-tasks expansion” before executing it.

- the fake `torch` spec was expanded so debug-mode CUDA diagnostics do not crash

The preview harness is in [run_preview.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py). The important last fix was making spoof patching reversible, so programmatic preview runs stop leaking monkeypatches into later tests in the same pytest process.

- `python scripts/preview/run_preview.py` and `python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --debug` both completed in about 19 seconds.
- `pytest tests/test_preview_harness.py -v` passed.
- `pytest tests/test_preview_smoke.py -m smoke -v` passed.
- Full `pytest tests/` finished at `677 passed / 3 skipped / 6 failed`.

> DEVELOPER

Show me how to run it in varous modes

> AGENT

From [run_preview.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py), run it from the worker repo root:

```bash
cd /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker
```

Normal mode, all default preview tasks:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py
```

Debug mode, all default preview tasks:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --debug
```

Force normal mode explicitly:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --normal
```

Only specific task types:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --task-types travel_orchestrator,qwen_image,z_image_turbo
```

Specific task types in debug:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --debug --task-types travel_orchestrator,join_clips_orchestrator,individual_travel_segment
```

Write the terminal output to a log file as well:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --output-log /tmp/preview.log
```

Debug + log file:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --debug --output-log /tmp/preview-debug.log
```

Control fake idle polling between tasks:
```bash
python scripts/preview/run_preview.py --idle-polls 0
```

The default fixture set is:
- `travel_orchestrator`
- `join_clips_orchestrator`
- `individual_travel_segment`
- `qwen_image`
- `z_image_turbo`

If you want, I can also add a tiny shell alias or Make target so this is easier to run repeatedly.

- added early interpreter handoff in [`reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py)

> AGENT

Yes, mostly.

It is not a fake slideshow. The preview harness runs the real worker entrypoints and task-processing code from the codebase:
- [`run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py#L429) calls the real [`process_single_task()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/runtime/worker/server.py#L145)
- the spoof queue calls the real [`process_task_impl()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/source/task_handlers/queue/task_processor.py#L60)
- orchestrator tasks go through the real task registry/orchestrator flow in `source/`
- your requested task types are built as real task payloads in [`fixtures.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/fixtures.py)

What is spoofed is the outside world and the heavy runtime boundary:
- DB reads/writes are replaced by the in-memory runtime in [`db_spoof.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/db_spoof.py#L25)
- network/download/upload calls are monkeypatched in [`run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py#L114)
- `wgp`, `torch`, and `huggingface_hub` are stubbed in [`wgp_spoof.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/wgp_spoof.py#L123)
- `HEADLESS_WAN2GP_SMOKE=1` is forced in [`run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py#L70), so it does not do real model downloads or real inference

So the accurate answer is: it runs the actual worker/orchestrator/task code paths, but with spoofed infra and smoke-mode generation. That makes it a good end-to-end preview of lifecycle and logs, not a true production execution.

If you want, the next improvement is a second mode: `preview --real-runtime`, where we keep the same harness but stop stubbing `wgp`/downloads and let it hit the actual local runtime.

> DEVELOPER

Is it well-structured?

> AGENT

I’m doing a quick structure review of the preview harness itself so I can answer on the code shape rather than just the behavior.

> AGENT

Yes, broadly. I’d call it well-structured enough for its purpose, but not especially elegant yet.

What’s good:
- The harness is split by responsibility instead of being one giant script:
  [`run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py),
  [`db_spoof.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/db_spoof.py),
  [`wgp_spoof.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/wgp_spoof.py),
  [`network_spoof.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/network_spoof.py),
  [`fixtures.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/fixtures.py).
- The split is the right one: fixtures, fake DB/runtime, fake model/runtime boundary, and runner/orchestration are separated.
- It drives real worker code paths rather than reimplementing business logic, which is the most important architectural choice.

What I would tighten:
- [`run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py) is too large at about 600 lines and owns too much: arg parsing, env prep, monkeypatch installation, preview-specific handler shims, and the main loop.
- The patch wiring in [`run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py#L120) is brittle because it relies on lots of dotted string targets. It works, but it will drift as imports move.
- [`SpoofQueue`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/wgp_spoof.py#L144) is a duck-typed stand-in for the real queue surface. That is pragmatic, but it is the part most likely to break silently when production queue behavior changes.

So the honest answer is:
- good structure at the module level
- slightly overloaded runner
- acceptable engineering for a preview harness
- not yet “clean framework” quality

If I were polishing it further, I’d do three refactors:
- move patch target registration into a dedicated `patch_registry.py`
- split [`run_preview.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-worker/scripts/preview/run_preview.py) into `runner.py` and `patches.py`
- define a small explicit queue protocol/interface so `SpoofQueue` is checked against a stable contract instead of ad hoc parity
